People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that a lot of the things that usually make me feel like me just started feeling kind of flat. Heavy, even. Like, I’m normally someone who will go out for photography walks, notice light on buildings, take my camera and make a whole afternoon of it. I also like mentoring the kids — that’s usually something that gives me a sense of purpose. And even keeping my calendar clean and organized, that’s always been part of how I keep myself together.

But lately I just… stopped initiating those things. Not because I decided they weren’t important, exactly. It’s more like the spark or the push isn’t there. I’ll think, “I should go take photos,” or “I should check in on the kids,” or “I need to straighten out my schedule,” and then it feels like trying to lift something too heavy. It’s been bothering me because those are the things I count on to feel grounded, and instead they just feel dull and hard to start.

Yeah, I have. That’s really the main thing that made me think, okay, something is off enough that I should talk to someone.

Photography walks used to be one of those things where I didn’t even have to convince myself — I’d just notice the light outside or think about a neighborhood I wanted to wander through, and I’d grab my camera. Same with mentoring the kids. It usually makes me feel useful, like I’m connected to something outside of myself. And my calendar… that probably sounds small, but keeping it clean and organized has always been one of the ways I feel steady.

Lately all of that has felt flat. Not like I hate those things now, exactly. More like there’s no pull toward them. I’ll think about doing them, and then it feels heavy, like starting takes way more effort than it should. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really tracked it as “energy” in a clear way. There are times where starting things feels heavy, like I was saying, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being physically tired or low-energy.

Like, I’m not sitting there thinking, “Wow, I’m exhausted,” or needing to lie down all the time. It’s more that I’ll look at something I normally would just do, even something not that demanding, and there’s this resistance to getting going. But I don’t want to say yes to being more tired if that’s not exactly what’s happening.

So… maybe I haven’t noticed it as tiredness. If anything, I’ve noticed the lack of initiative more than actual fatigue.

as clearly as if someone is beside me, but when I look there’s no one there.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: I have stretches where I feel unusually upbeat and sure everything is clicking, then a tiny change like someone moving a meeting can make me sharp with people in a way that surprises me.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: During those stretches I start rebuilding work templates, making care plans, emailing relatives, and reorganizing files all at once, like sitting still would mean I’m dropping the ball.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I’ll feel like I’m the only one who can straighten out the doctors, the family, and the office, and I start believing my system is better than anyone else’s judgment.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: There have been nights when I sleep two or three hours, wake up before dawn, and feel ready to answer emails or update appointment lists instead of feeling tired.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: My wife and coworkers tell me I talk so fast during those periods that I jump from medical forms to work deadlines to mentoring ideas before they can get a word in.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: During my high-energy stretches, the certainty that messages or calendar problems meant something special would get stronger, and I would sometimes hear those short comments while I was running on very little sleep.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  manifestation: For most of this whole period, I’ve been cycling back into those driven, overconfident stretches where I sleep less and take on too much, even if some weeks are quieter.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode\n  manifestation: There have been calm weeks when I wasn’t revved up or sleeping less, but for more than a couple of weeks I still heard my name or felt convinced ordinary work comments were coded tests.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My work has stayed intact overall, but I’ve missed calendar details and had to double-check tasks that used to be automatic. I’ve also pulled back from photography outings and mentoring because I don’t feel as present or reliable.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 years and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and three months, starting after my family member’s health and behavior changed and I took on more coordination.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 10 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the fixed worries about signs in calendars or meetings have been happening for roughly ten months.\n- key: manic_episode_duration\n  name: Manic Episode Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: The elevated, driven stretches with less sleep and too much confidence have been recurring for about eleven months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / executive_secretary_or_executive_administrative_assistant / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S023\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest or enjoyment in photography walks, mentoring the kids, or organizing your calendar?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. That’s really the main thing that made me think, okay, something is off enough that I should talk to someone.\n\nPhotography walks used to be one of those things where I didn’t even have to convince myself — I’d just notice the light outside or think about a neighborhood I wanted to wander through, and I’d grab my camera. Same with mentoring the kids. It usually makes me feel useful, like I’m connected to something outside of myself. And my calendar… that probably sounds small, but keeping it clean and organized has always been one of the ways I feel steady.\n\nLately all of that has felt flat. Not like I hate those things now, exactly. More like there’s no pull toward them. I’ll think about doing them, and then it feels heavy, like starting takes way more effort than it should. So I just don’t initiate it, or I put it off, and then I feel bothered because those are the things that usually make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the time, even when you’re not thinking about the things you’ve lost interest in?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d call it sad most of the time. I mean, I know I’ve been bothered by feeling flat, and there are moments where I get kind of discouraged about it, like, “Why can’t I just do the thing I normally do?” But if you mean am I walking around feeling actively sad or down all day, separate from the stuff I’ve lost interest in… I don’t know that I’ve noticed that clearly.\n\nIt feels more like an absence, if that makes sense. Like the volume is turned down. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m sad most of the time if that’s not exactly what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or low on energy lately, even when you’re not doing anything physically taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really tracked it as “energy” in a clear way. There are times where starting things feels heavy, like I was saying, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being physically tired or low-energy.\n\nLike, I’m not sitting there thinking, “Wow, I’m exhausted,” or needing to lie down all the time. It’s more that I’ll look at something I normally would just do, even something not that demanding, and there’s this resistance to getting going. But I don’t want to say yes to being more tired if that’s not exactly what’s happening.\n\nSo… maybe I haven’t noticed it as tiredness. If anything, I’ve noticed the lack of initiative more than actual fatigue.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that your mood has been down or low even when you're not thinking about the things you've lost interest in or the effort it takes to start them?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m still not sure I can separate it that cleanly. Like, when you ask it that way — “is my mood low even when I’m not thinking about the hobbies or the effort?” — I want to be accurate, because I don’t want to say yes just because things have been off.

If I’m just sitting there not thinking about any of that, I don’t necessarily feel sad. I might just feel… neutral, maybe too neutral.

So I guess my answer is I haven’t clearly noticed a separate low mood. It’s more tied to realizing I’m not getting much out of things or not starting them.

me I talk so fast during those periods that I jump from medical forms to work deadlines to mentoring ideas before they can get a word in.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: During my high-energy stretches, the certainty that messages or calendar problems meant something special would get stronger, and I would sometimes hear those short comments while I was running on very little sleep.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  manifestation: For most of this whole period, I’ve been cycling back into those driven, overconfident stretches where I sleep less and take on too much, even if some weeks are quieter.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: My work has stayed intact overall, but I’ve missed calendar details and had to double-check tasks that used to be automatic. I mean, I know I’ve been bothered by feeling flat, and there are moments where I get kind of discouraged about it, like, “Why can’t I just do the thing I normally do?” But if you mean am I walking around feeling actively sad or down all day, separate from the stuff I’ve lost interest in… I don’t know that I’ve noticed that clearly.\n\nIt feels more like an absence, if that makes sense. Like the volume is turned down. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m sad most of the time if that’s not exactly what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or low on energy lately, even when you’re not doing anything physically taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really tracked it as “energy” in a clear way. I might just feel… neutral, maybe too neutral.\n\nSo I guess my answer is I haven’t clearly noticed a separate low mood. I don’t think so… not in a strong way, anyway. I mean, I can get annoyed with myself when I don’t follow through on something the way I think I should, especially if it’s something simple or something I normally handle without making a big production out of it. I’ll have a thought like, “Come on, why is this taking you so long?” But then it sort of passes, and I can usually recognize that it’s just me being frustrated.

So… no, I haven’t really noticed a big guilt thing or feeling worthless for no clear reason. If anything, it’s more mild frustration with myself than feeling like I’m not living up to some major standard.

for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: There have been nights when I sleep two or three hours, wake up before dawn, and feel ready to answer emails or update appointment lists instead of feeling tired.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: My wife and coworkers tell me I talk so fast during those periods that I jump from medical forms to work deadlines to mentoring ideas before they can get a word in.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: During my high-energy stretches, the certainty that messages or calendar problems meant something special would get stronger, and I would sometimes hear those short comments while I was running on very little sleep.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  manifestation: For most of this whole period, I’ve been cycling back into those driven, overconfident stretches where I sleep less and take on too much, even if some weeks are quieter.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode\n  manifestation: There have been calm weeks when I wasn’t revved up or sleeping less, but for more than a couple of weeks I still heard my name or felt convinced ordinary work comments were coded tests.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My work has stayed intact overall, but I’ve missed calendar details and had to double-check tasks that used to be automatic. I’ve also pulled back from photography outings and mentoring because I don’t feel as present or reliable.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 years and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and three months, starting after my family member’s health and behavior changed and I took on more coordination.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 10 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the fixed worries about signs in calendars or meetings have been happening for roughly ten months.\n- key: manic_episode_duration\n  name: Manic Episode Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: The elevated, driven stretches with less sleep and too much confidence have been recurring for about eleven months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / executive_secretary_or_executive_administrative_assistant / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S023\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or low on energy lately, even when you’re not doing anything physically taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really tracked it as “energy” in a clear way. There are times where starting things feels heavy, like I was saying, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being physically tired or low-energy.\n\nLike, I’m not sitting there thinking, “Wow, I’m exhausted,” or needing to lie down all the time. It’s more that I’ll look at something I normally would just do, even something not that demanding, and there’s this resistance to getting going. But I don’t want to say yes to being more tired if that’s not exactly what’s happening.\n\nSo… maybe I haven’t noticed it as tiredness. If anything, I’ve noticed the lack of initiative more than actual fatigue.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood has been down or low even when you're not thinking about the things you've lost interest in or the effort it takes to start them?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can separate it that cleanly. Like, when you ask it that way — “is my mood low even when I’m not thinking about the hobbies or the effort?” — I want to be accurate, because I don’t want to say yes just because things have been off.\n\nThere are moments where I’m kind of muted, I guess. But I don’t know that I’d describe my actual mood as down or low on its own. It’s more like I notice the blankness when something should feel engaging, and then I react to that with frustration or disappointment. If I’m just sitting there not thinking about any of that, I don’t necessarily feel sad. I might just feel… neutral, maybe too neutral.\n\nSo I guess my answer is I haven’t clearly noticed a separate low mood. It’s more tied to realizing I’m not getting much out of things or not starting them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not living up to your own standards or that you're letting people down, even if there's no clear reason to think that's true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a strong way, anyway. I mean, I can get annoyed with myself when I don’t follow through on something the way I think I should, especially if it’s something simple or something I normally handle without making a big production out of it. But I don’t know that I’d call it feeling like I’m letting people down.\n\nLike, I’m not walking around thinking, “I’m a failure,” or assuming everyone’s disappointed in me. It’s more occasional self-criticism, I guess. I’ll have a thought like, “Come on, why is this taking you so long?” But then it sort of passes, and I can usually recognize that it’s just me being frustrated.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a big guilt thing or feeling worthless for no clear reason. If anything, it’s more mild frustration with myself than feeling like I’m not living up to some major standard.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or that even small tasks take longer to complete because your thoughts or actions feel slowed down?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I haven’t really noticed that, no. Or at least not in a way where I’d say my movements or speech feel slowed down. I don’t think people have been telling me I’m talking slower, and I don’t feel like I’m physically dragging through the room or anything like that.

Small tasks can take longer sometimes, but I think that’s more because I hesitate getting started or I get stuck deciding how to approach them, not because my body or thoughts are moving in slow motion. Like once I’m actually doing the thing, I don’t usually feel slowed down. It’s more the lead-up that can stretch out.

So I’d say… no, I haven’t clearly noticed being slowed down in that specific sense.

I’ve also pulled back from photography outings and mentoring because I don’t feel as present or reliable.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 years and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and three months, starting after my family member’s health and behavior changed and I took on more coordination.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 10 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the fixed worries about signs in calendars or meetings have been happening for roughly ten months.\n- key: manic_episode_duration\n  name: Manic Episode Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: The elevated, driven stretches with less sleep and too much confidence have been recurring for about eleven months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / executive_secretary_or_executive_administrative_assistant / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S023\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood has been down or low even when you're not thinking about the things you've lost interest in or the effort it takes to start them?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can separate it that cleanly. But I don’t know that I’d describe my actual mood as down or low on its own. It’s more like I notice the blankness when something should feel engaging, and then I react to that with frustration or disappointment. If I’m just sitting there not thinking about any of that, I don’t necessarily feel sad. I might just feel… neutral, maybe too neutral.\n\nSo I guess my answer is I haven’t clearly noticed a separate low mood. But I don’t know that I’d call it feeling like I’m letting people down.\n\nLike, I’m not walking around thinking, “I’m a failure,” or assuming everyone’s disappointed in me. It’s more occasional self-criticism, I guess. I’ll have a thought like, “Come on, why is this taking you so long?” But then it sort of passes, and I can usually recognize that it’s just me being frustrated.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a big guilt thing or feeling worthless for no clear reason. If anything, it’s more mild frustration with myself than feeling like I’m not living up to some major standard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or that even small tasks take longer to complete because your thoughts or actions feel slowed down?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed that, no. Or at least not in a way where I’d say my movements or speech feel slowed down. I don’t think people have been telling me I’m talking slower, and I don’t feel like I’m physically dragging through the room or anything like that.\n\nSmall tasks can take longer sometimes, but I think that’s more because I hesitate getting started or I get stuck deciding how to approach them, not because my body or thoughts are moving in slow motion. Like once I’m actually doing the thing, I don’t usually feel slowed down. It’s more the lead-up that can stretch out.\n\nSo I’d say… no, I haven’t clearly noticed being slowed down in that specific sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately, like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major there. My appetite seems… mostly the same, at least as far as I can tell. I’m not skipping meals because food seems disgusting or anything like that, and I haven’t had that thing where my clothes are suddenly looser or I’m looking at the scale surprised.

I mean, day to day it varies a little — some days I might eat lighter or get distracted and eat later than I meant to — but that feels pretty normal for me. I wouldn’t say I’m eating much less than usual, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight or noticing weight coming off. So, no, not really any clear appetite or weight change lately.

But I don’t know that I’d call it feeling like I’m letting people down.\n\nLike, I’m not walking around thinking, “I’m a failure,” or assuming everyone’s disappointed in me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. It’s not that I suddenly hate those things or anything. It’s more like they’ve gone kind of flat on me.

Photography walks used to be one of those things that made me feel like myself. I’d notice light hitting a building a certain way, or I’d plan a route just because I knew there’d be interesting angles or people or shadows. Lately, even thinking about getting the camera ready feels heavy, like there’s this extra step between me and doing it. And if I do go, I’m kind of going through the motions. I’ll see something I know I would normally care about, and there’s just not much spark there.

Same with mentoring the kids. I used to get a lot of satisfaction from that, like I was useful and present and connected. Now I still know it matters, intellectually, but I don’t feel pulled toward it the same way. I stop initiating. I don’t reach out as much. I’ll put it off and then feel weird about how little energy I have for something I genuinely value.

Even smaller things, like keeping a clean calendar — and I know that sounds boring, but for me that’s always been part of feeling steady and competent — even that has started to feel like a chore instead of something that gives me a sense of order. So yes, the pleasure or satisfaction is dulled. Flat is probably the best word. Flat and heavy.

I mean, I know I’ve been bothered by feeling flat, and there are moments where I get kind of discouraged about it, like, “Why can’t I just do the thing I normally do?” But if you mean am I walking around feeling actively sad or down all day, separate from the stuff I’ve lost interest in… I don’t know that I’ve noticed that clearly.\n\nIt feels more like an absence, if that makes sense. Like the volume is turned down. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m sad most of the time if that’s not exactly what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or low on energy lately, even when you’re not doing anything physically taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really tracked it as “energy” in a clear way. It’s more that I’ll look at something I normally would just do, even something not that demanding, and there’s this resistance to getting going. If anything, I’ve noticed the lack of initiative more than actual fatigue.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood has been down or low even when you're not thinking about the things you've lost interest in or the effort it takes to start them?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can separate it that cleanly. I might just feel… neutral, maybe too neutral.\n\nSo I guess my answer is I haven’t clearly noticed a separate low mood. But I don’t know that I’d call it feeling like I’m letting people down.\n\nLike, I’m not walking around thinking, “I’m a failure,” or assuming everyone’s disappointed in me. I’ll have a thought like, “Come on, why is this taking you so long?” But then it sort of passes, and I can usually recognize that it’s just me being frustrated.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a big guilt thing or feeling worthless for no clear reason. If anything, it’s more mild frustration with myself than feeling like I’m not living up to some major standard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or that even small tasks take longer to complete because your thoughts or actions feel slowed down?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed that, no. I wouldn’t say I’m eating much less than usual, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight or noticing weight coming off. I’m not completely sure how to measure that, honestly. I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around every single day thinking, “I’m depressed” or “my mood is low,” in this very clear way. It’s more… some days feel dimmer than others, if that makes sense.

Over the last few weeks, I’ve had stretches where I feel kind of heavy or discouraged for a good part of the day, but I have trouble separating whether that’s actually low mood or just being tired, or frustrated, or not feeling much from things. It’s not like a quick bad mood that passes in ten minutes, but I also don’t know that I’d call it constant.

If I had to guess, I’d say it’s happened on some days, maybe several, but I’m not confident enough to give you a clean number. I notice it more when I stop and think about how my day felt overall, rather than in the moment. So… yes, maybe there have been days like that, but I’m not sure how often exactly.

And my calendar… that probably sounds small, but keeping it clean and organized has always been one of the ways I feel steady.\n\nLately all of that has felt flat. Not like I hate those things now, exactly. More like there’s no pull toward them. I’ll think about doing them, and then it feels heavy, like starting takes way more effort than it should. So I just don’t initiate it, or I put it off, and then I feel bothered because those are the things that usually make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the time, even when you’re not thinking about the things you’ve lost interest in?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d call it sad most of the time. I mean, I know I’ve been bothered by feeling flat, and there are moments where I get kind of discouraged about it, like, “Why can’t I just do the thing I normally do?” But if you mean am I walking around feeling actively sad or down all day, separate from the stuff I’ve lost interest in… I don’t know that I’ve noticed that clearly.\n\nIt feels more like an absence, if that makes sense. Like the volume is turned down. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m sad most of the time if that’s not exactly what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or low on energy lately, even when you’re not doing anything physically taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really tracked it as “energy” in a clear way. There are times where starting things feels heavy, like I was saying, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being physically tired or low-energy.\n\nLike, I’m not sitting there thinking, “Wow, I’m exhausted,” or needing to lie down all the time. It’s more that I’ll look at something I normally would just do, even something not that demanding, and there’s this resistance to getting going. But I don’t want to say yes to being more tired if that’s not exactly what’s happening.\n\nSo… maybe I haven’t noticed it as tiredness. If anything, I’ve noticed the lack of initiative more than actual fatigue.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood has been down or low even when you're not thinking about the things you've lost interest in or the effort it takes to start them?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can separate it that cleanly. Like, when you ask it that way — “is my mood low even when I’m not thinking about the hobbies or the effort?” — I want to be accurate, because I don’t want to say yes just because things have been off.\n\nThere are moments where I’m kind of muted, I guess. But I don’t know that I’d describe my actual mood as down or low on its own. It’s more like I notice the blankness when something should feel engaging, and then I react to that with frustration or disappointment. If I’m just sitting there not thinking about any of that, I don’t necessarily feel sad. I might just feel… neutral, maybe too neutral.\n\nSo I guess my answer is I haven’t clearly noticed a separate low mood. It’s more tied to realizing I’m not getting much out of things or not starting them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not living up to your own standards or that you're letting people down, even if there's no clear reason to think that's true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a strong way, anyway. I mean, I can get annoyed with myself when I don’t follow through on something the way I think I should, especially if it’s something simple or something I normally handle without making a big production out of it. But I don’t know that I’d call it feeling like I’m letting people down.\n\nLike, I’m not walking around thinking, “I’m a failure,” or assuming everyone’s disappointed in me. It’s more occasional self-criticism, I guess. I’ll have a thought like, “Come on, why is this taking you so long?” But then it sort of passes, and I can usually recognize that it’s just me being frustrated.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a big guilt thing or feeling worthless for no clear reason. If anything, it’s more mild frustration with myself than feeling like I’m not living up to some major standard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or that even small tasks take longer to complete because your thoughts or actions feel slowed down?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed that, no. Or at least not in a way where I’d say my movements or speech feel slowed down. I don’t think people have been telling me I’m talking slower, and I don’t feel like I’m physically dragging through the room or anything like that.\n\nSmall tasks can take longer sometimes, but I think that’s more because I hesitate getting started or I get stuck deciding how to approach them, not because my body or thoughts are moving in slow motion. Like once I’m actually doing the thing, I don’t usually feel slowed down. It’s more the lead-up that can stretch out.\n\nSo I’d say… no, I haven’t clearly noticed being slowed down in that specific sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately, like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major there. My appetite seems… mostly the same, at least as far as I can tell. I’m not skipping meals because food seems disgusting or anything like that, and I haven’t had that thing where my clothes are suddenly looser or I’m looking at the scale surprised.\n\nI mean, day to day it varies a little — some days I might eat lighter or get distracted and eat later than I meant to — but that feels pretty normal for me. I wouldn’t say I’m eating much less than usual, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight or noticing weight coming off. So, no, not really any clear appetite or weight change lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like photography walks or mentoring, don’t give you the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. It’s not that I suddenly hate those things or anything. It’s more like they’ve gone kind of flat on me.\n\nPhotography walks used to be one of those things that made me feel like myself. I’d notice light hitting a building a certain way, or I’d plan a route just because I knew there’d be interesting angles or people or shadows. Lately, even thinking about getting the camera ready feels heavy, like there’s this extra step between me and doing it. And if I do go, I’m kind of going through the motions. I’ll see something I know I would normally care about, and there’s just not much spark there.\n\nSame with mentoring the kids. I used to get a lot of satisfaction from that, like I was useful and present and connected. Now I still know it matters, intellectually, but I don’t feel pulled toward it the same way. I stop initiating. I don’t reach out as much. I’ll put it off and then feel weird about how little energy I have for something I genuinely value.\n\nEven smaller things, like keeping a clean calendar — and I know that sounds boring, but for me that’s always been part of feeling steady and competent — even that has started to feel like a chore instead of something that gives me a sense of order. So yes, the pleasure or satisfaction is dulled. Flat is probably the best word. Flat and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where your mood has felt persistently down or low — not just a passing feeling — and if so, how often has that happened over the last few weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to measure that, honestly. I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around every single day thinking, “I’m depressed” or “my mood is low,” in this very clear way. It’s more… some days feel dimmer than others, if that makes sense.\n\nOver the last few weeks, I’ve had stretches where I feel kind of heavy or discouraged for a good part of the day, but I have trouble separating whether that’s actually low mood or just being tired, or frustrated, or not feeling much from things. It’s not like a quick bad mood that passes in ten minutes, but I also don’t know that I’d call it constant.\n\nIf I had to guess, I’d say it’s happened on some days, maybe several, but I’m not confident enough to give you a clean number. I notice it more when I stop and think about how my day felt overall, rather than in the moment. So… yes, maybe there have been days like that, but I’m not sure how often exactly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I wouldn’t say I’m eating much less than usual, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight or noticing weight coming off. So, no, not really any clear appetite or weight change lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like photography walks or mentoring, don’t give you the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. It’s not that I suddenly hate those things or anything. It’s more like they’ve gone kind of flat on me.\n\nPhotography walks used to be one of those things that made me feel like myself. I’d notice light hitting a building a certain way, or I’d plan a route just because I knew there’d be interesting angles or people or shadows. Lately, even thinking about getting the camera ready feels heavy, like there’s this extra step between me and doing it. And if I do go, I’m kind of going through the motions. I’ll see something I know I would normally care about, and there’s just not much spark there.\n\nSame with mentoring the kids. I used to get a lot of satisfaction from that, like I was useful and present and connected. Now I still know it matters, intellectually, but I don’t feel pulled toward it the same way. I’ll put it off and then feel weird about how little energy I have for something I genuinely value.\n\nEven smaller things, like keeping a clean calendar — and I know that sounds boring, but for me that’s always been part of feeling steady and competent — even that has started to feel like a chore instead of something that gives me a sense of order. So yes, the pleasure or satisfaction is dulled. Flat is probably the best word. Flat and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where your mood has felt persistently down or low — not just a passing feeling — and if so, how often has that happened over the last few weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to measure that, honestly. It’s more… some days feel dimmer than others, if that makes sense.\n\nOver the last few weeks, I’ve had stretches where I feel kind of heavy or discouraged for a good part of the day, but I have trouble separating whether that’s actually low mood or just being tired, or frustrated, or not feeling much from things. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. I mean, I can be self-critical, sure — especially if I forget something or don’t handle something as smoothly as I think I should. I’ve always had a bit of that “I should be more organized, I should be more on top of things” voice in my head.

But I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling like I’m fundamentally not good enough, or like I’m failing as a person even when nothing is actually going wrong. That doesn’t really fit. If I feel bad about myself, it’s usually tied to something specific, like “I didn’t follow through on that,” not this broad sense that I’m worthless or falling short of who I’m supposed to be.

So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a major thing lately. At least not beyond normal frustration with myself here and there.

Like the volume is turned down. I haven’t really tracked it as “energy” in a clear way. I mean, I can get annoyed with myself when I don’t follow through on something the way I think I should, especially if it’s something simple or something I normally handle without making a big production out of it. But I don’t know that I’d call it feeling like I’m letting people down.\n\nLike, I’m not walking around thinking, “I’m a failure,” or assuming everyone’s disappointed in me. It’s more occasional self-criticism, I guess. I’ll have a thought like, “Come on, why is this taking you so long?” But then it sort of passes, and I can usually recognize that it’s just me being frustrated.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a big guilt thing or feeling worthless for no clear reason. If anything, it’s more mild frustration with myself than feeling like I’m not living up to some major standard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or that even small tasks take longer to complete because your thoughts or actions feel slowed down?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed that, no. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, exactly. Even when I do manage to do them, it’s like the part of me that used to get something back from it just isn’t really coming online.

Like with the photography walks, I can physically go out, I can bring the camera, I can notice things that I know are interesting — a reflection, a doorway, somebody standing in good light — but it doesn’t land the same way. It’s more like I’m observing myself doing the activity rather than actually enjoying it. And that’s strange, because that used to make me feel like myself.

The mentoring is similar. I still care about the kids, and I know it matters. It’s not that I think it’s pointless. But the satisfaction, the sense of connection or purpose I used to feel afterward, it’s muted. I’ll do it and then come home feeling more tired than fulfilled.

And even something simple like keeping my calendar clean — which sounds small, but for me has always been a way of feeling steady and competent — that’s become heavy too. I’m not getting that little feeling of order or relief from it anymore.

So, yes. That’s really what made me come in. The things that usually anchor me or make me feel like myself started feeling flat and heavy, and then I noticed I was just not initiating them as much.

That doesn’t really fit. If I feel bad about myself, it’s usually tied to something specific, like “I didn’t follow through on that,” not this broad sense that I’m worthless or falling short of who I’m supposed to be.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a major thing lately. At least not beyond normal frustration with myself here and there.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like photography walks or mentoring the kids, even when you do manage to do them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Even when I do manage to do them, it’s like the part of me that used to get something back from it just isn’t really coming online.\n\nLike with the photography walks, I can physically go out, I can bring the camera, I can notice things that I know are interesting — a reflection, a doorway, somebody standing in good light — but it doesn’t land the same way. It’s more like I’m observing myself doing the activity rather than actually enjoying it. And that’s strange, because that used to make me feel like myself.\n\nThe mentoring is similar. I still care about the kids, and I know it matters. It’s not that I think it’s pointless. But the satisfaction, the sense of connection or purpose I used to feel afterward, it’s muted. I’ll do it and then come home feeling more tired than fulfilled.\n\nAnd even something simple like keeping my calendar clean — which sounds small, but for me has always been a way of feeling steady and competent — that’s become heavy too. I’m not getting that little feeling of order or relief from it anymore.\n\nSo, yes. That’s really what made me come in. The things that usually anchor me or make me feel like myself started feeling flat and heavy, and then I noticed I was just not initiating them as much.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that even when you push yourself to do the things you used to enjoy, like photography walks or mentoring, they still feel meaningless or unfulfilling?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / executive_secretary_or_executive_administrative_assistant / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Negative_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"What made me come in is that the things that usually make me feel like myself—photography walks, mentoring the kids, even keeping a clean calendar—started feeling flat and heavy, and I just stopped initiating them.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… that’s pretty much it. I can still make myself do them sometimes, but they don’t give back what they used to.

With the photography walks, for example, I’ll go through the motions — take the camera, walk the same kinds of routes, notice little things I would normally want to capture — but it feels flat. Like I know, intellectually, “This is the kind of thing I enjoy,” but I’m not really feeling it. It’s almost heavy instead of restorative.

And with mentoring the kids, I still know it matters, and I don’t want to sound like I don’t care, because I do. But the feeling afterward used to be, I don’t know, grounding? Like I was connected to something useful. Lately it’s more like I did the task and then I’m just drained.

Even keeping my calendar clean — which is a small thing, but it usually makes me feel like myself, like I have some order — has started to feel like another weight. That’s actually what made me come in: the things that normally anchor me started feeling flat and heavy, and then I noticed I wasn’t even starting them as much.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that a lot of the things that usually make me feel like me just started feeling kind of flat. Heavy, even. Like, I’m normally someone who will go out for photography walks, notice light on buildings, take my camera and make a whole afternoon of it. I also like mentoring the kids — that’s usually something that gives me a sense of purpose. And even keeping my calendar clean and organized, that’s always been part of how I keep myself together.\n\nBut lately I just… stopped initiating those things. Not because I decided they weren’t important, exactly. It’s more like the spark or the push isn’t there. I’ll think, “I should go take photos,” or “I should check in on the kids,” or “I need to straighten out my schedule,” and then it feels like trying to lift something too heavy. It’s been bothering me because those are the things I count on to feel grounded, and instead they just feel dull and hard to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest or enjoyment in photography walks, mentoring the kids, or organizing your calendar?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. That’s really the main thing that made me think, okay, something is off enough that I should talk to someone.\n\nPhotography walks used to be one of those things where I didn’t even have to convince myself — I’d just notice the light outside or think about a neighborhood I wanted to wander through, and I’d grab my camera. Same with mentoring the kids. It usually makes me feel useful, like I’m connected to something outside of myself. And my calendar… that probably sounds small, but keeping it clean and organized has always been one of the ways I feel steady.\n\nLately all of that has felt flat. Not like I hate those things now, exactly. More like there’s no pull toward them. I’ll think about doing them, and then it feels heavy, like starting takes way more effort than it should. So I just don’t initiate it, or I put it off, and then I feel bothered because those are the things that usually make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the time, even when you’re not thinking about the things you’ve lost interest in?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d call it sad most of the time. I mean, I know I’ve been bothered by feeling flat, and there are moments where I get kind of discouraged about it, like, “Why can’t I just do the thing I normally do?” But if you mean am I walking around feeling actively sad or down all day, separate from the stuff I’ve lost interest in… I don’t know that I’ve noticed that clearly.\n\nIt feels more like an absence, if that makes sense. Like the volume is turned down. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m sad most of the time if that’s not exactly what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or low on energy lately, even when you’re not doing anything physically taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really tracked it as “energy” in a clear way. There are times where starting things feels heavy, like I was saying, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being physically tired or low-energy.\n\nLike, I’m not sitting there thinking, “Wow, I’m exhausted,” or needing to lie down all the time. It’s more that I’ll look at something I normally would just do, even something not that demanding, and there’s this resistance to getting going. But I don’t want to say yes to being more tired if that’s not exactly what’s happening.\n\nSo… maybe I haven’t noticed it as tiredness. If anything, I’ve noticed the lack of initiative more than actual fatigue.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood has been down or low even when you're not thinking about the things you've lost interest in or the effort it takes to start them?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can separate it that cleanly. Like, when you ask it that way — “is my mood low even when I’m not thinking about the hobbies or the effort?” — I want to be accurate, because I don’t want to say yes just because things have been off.\n\nThere are moments where I’m kind of muted, I guess. But I don’t know that I’d describe my actual mood as down or low on its own. It’s more like I notice the blankness when something should feel engaging, and then I react to that with frustration or disappointment. If I’m just sitting there not thinking about any of that, I don’t necessarily feel sad. I might just feel… neutral, maybe too neutral.\n\nSo I guess my answer is I haven’t clearly noticed a separate low mood. It’s more tied to realizing I’m not getting much out of things or not starting them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not living up to your own standards or that you're letting people down, even if there's no clear reason to think that's true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a strong way, anyway. I mean, I can get annoyed with myself when I don’t follow through on something the way I think I should, especially if it’s something simple or something I normally handle without making a big production out of it. But I don’t know that I’d call it feeling like I’m letting people down.\n\nLike, I’m not walking around thinking, “I’m a failure,” or assuming everyone’s disappointed in me. It’s more occasional self-criticism, I guess. I’ll have a thought like, “Come on, why is this taking you so long?” But then it sort of passes, and I can usually recognize that it’s just me being frustrated.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a big guilt thing or feeling worthless for no clear reason. If anything, it’s more mild frustration with myself than feeling like I’m not living up to some major standard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or that even small tasks take longer to complete because your thoughts or actions feel slowed down?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed that, no. Or at least not in a way where I’d say my movements or speech feel slowed down. I don’t think people have been telling me I’m talking slower, and I don’t feel like I’m physically dragging through the room or anything like that.\n\nSmall tasks can take longer sometimes, but I think that’s more because I hesitate getting started or I get stuck deciding how to approach them, not because my body or thoughts are moving in slow motion. Like once I’m actually doing the thing, I don’t usually feel slowed down. It’s more the lead-up that can stretch out.\n\nSo I’d say… no, I haven’t clearly noticed being slowed down in that specific sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately, like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major there. My appetite seems… mostly the same, at least as far as I can tell. I’m not skipping meals because food seems disgusting or anything like that, and I haven’t had that thing where my clothes are suddenly looser or I’m looking at the scale surprised.\n\nI mean, day to day it varies a little — some days I might eat lighter or get distracted and eat later than I meant to — but that feels pretty normal for me. I wouldn’t say I’m eating much less than usual, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight or noticing weight coming off. So, no, not really any clear appetite or weight change lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like photography walks or mentoring, don’t give you the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. It’s not that I suddenly hate those things or anything. It’s more like they’ve gone kind of flat on me.\n\nPhotography walks used to be one of those things that made me feel like myself. I’d notice light hitting a building a certain way, or I’d plan a route just because I knew there’d be interesting angles or people or shadows. Lately, even thinking about getting the camera ready feels heavy, like there’s this extra step between me and doing it. And if I do go, I’m kind of going through the motions. I’ll see something I know I would normally care about, and there’s just not much spark there.\n\nSame with mentoring the kids. I used to get a lot of satisfaction from that, like I was useful and present and connected. Now I still know it matters, intellectually, but I don’t feel pulled toward it the same way. I stop initiating. I don’t reach out as much. I’ll put it off and then feel weird about how little energy I have for something I genuinely value.\n\nEven smaller things, like keeping a clean calendar — and I know that sounds boring, but for me that’s always been part of feeling steady and competent — even that has started to feel like a chore instead of something that gives me a sense of order. So yes, the pleasure or satisfaction is dulled. Flat is probably the best word. Flat and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where your mood has felt persistently down or low — not just a passing feeling — and if so, how often has that happened over the last few weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to measure that, honestly. I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around every single day thinking, “I’m depressed” or “my mood is low,” in this very clear way. It’s more… some days feel dimmer than others, if that makes sense.\n\nOver the last few weeks, I’ve had stretches where I feel kind of heavy or discouraged for a good part of the day, but I have trouble separating whether that’s actually low mood or just being tired, or frustrated, or not feeling much from things. It’s not like a quick bad mood that passes in ten minutes, but I also don’t know that I’d call it constant.\n\nIf I had to guess, I’d say it’s happened on some days, maybe several, but I’m not confident enough to give you a clean number. I notice it more when I stop and think about how my day felt overall, rather than in the moment. So… yes, maybe there have been days like that, but I’m not sure how often exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re not good enough or that you’re falling short of who you feel like you should be, even when things aren’t going badly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. I mean, I can be self-critical, sure — especially if I forget something or don’t handle something as smoothly as I think I should. I’ve always had a bit of that “I should be more organized, I should be more on top of things” voice in my head.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling like I’m fundamentally not good enough, or like I’m failing as a person even when nothing is actually going wrong. It’s more… some days feel dimmer than others, if that makes sense.\n\nOver the last few weeks, I’ve had stretches where I feel kind of heavy or discouraged for a good part of the day, but I have trouble separating whether that’s actually low mood or just being tired, or frustrated, or not feeling much from things. It’s not like a quick bad mood that passes in ten minutes, but I also don’t know that I’d call it constant.\n\nIf I had to guess, I’d say it’s happened on some days, maybe several, but I’m not confident enough to give you a clean number. I notice it more when I stop and think about how my day felt overall, rather than in the moment. So… yes, maybe there have been days like that, but I’m not sure how often exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re not good enough or that you’re falling short of who you feel like you should be, even when things aren’t going badly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. I mean, I can be self-critical, sure — especially if I forget something or don’t handle something as smoothly as I think I should. I’ve always had a bit of that “I should be more organized, I should be more on top of things” voice in my head.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling like I’m fundamentally not good enough, or like I’m failing as a person even when nothing is actually going wrong. That doesn’t really fit. If I feel bad about myself, it’s usually tied to something specific, like “I didn’t follow through on that,” not this broad sense that I’m worthless or falling short of who I’m supposed to be.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a major thing lately. At least not beyond normal frustration with myself here and there.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like photography walks or mentoring the kids, even when you do manage to do them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Even when I do manage to do them, it’s like the part of me that used to get something back from it just isn’t really coming online.\n\nLike with the photography walks, I can physically go out, I can bring the camera, I can notice things that I know are interesting — a reflection, a doorway, somebody standing in good light — but it doesn’t land the same way. It’s more like I’m observing myself doing the activity rather than actually enjoying it. And that’s strange, because that used to make me feel like myself.\n\nThe mentoring is similar. I still care about the kids, and I know it matters. It’s not that I think it’s pointless. But the satisfaction, the sense of connection or purpose I used to feel afterward, it’s muted. I’ll do it and then come home feeling more tired than fulfilled.\n\nAnd even something simple like keeping my calendar clean — which sounds small, but for me has always been a way of feeling steady and competent — that’s become heavy too. I’m not getting that little feeling of order or relief from it anymore.\n\nSo, yes. That’s really what made me come in. The things that usually anchor me or make me feel like myself started feeling flat and heavy, and then I noticed I was just not initiating them as much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you push yourself to do the things you used to enjoy, like photography walks or mentoring, they still feel meaningless or unfulfilling?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much it. I can still make myself do them sometimes, but they don’t give back what they used to.\n\nWith the photography walks, for example, I’ll go through the motions — take the camera, walk the same kinds of routes, notice little things I would normally want to capture — but it feels flat. Like I know, intellectually, “This is the kind of thing I enjoy,” but I’m not really feeling it. It’s almost heavy instead of restorative.\n\nAnd with mentoring the kids, I still know it matters, and I don’t want to sound like I don’t care, because I do. But the feeling afterward used to be, I don’t know, grounding? Like I was connected to something useful. Lately it’s more like I did the task and then I’m just drained.\n\nEven keeping my calendar clean — which is a small thing, but it usually makes me feel like myself, like I have some order — has started to feel like another weight. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been… longer than I wanted to admit at first. I’d say about a year and three months now, give or take.

It wasn’t like I woke up one day and everything felt flat. It kind of crept in after my family member’s health and behavior started changing, and I ended up taking on more of the coordination around that — appointments, calls, keeping track of things, trying to make sure nothing fell through the cracks. At first I thought, “Okay, I’m just tired, this is a stressful period,” and I kept telling myself it would pass once things settled.

But it didn’t really settle, and after a while I noticed that the heaviness wasn’t just about the coordination itself. It was bleeding into the things that usually helped me feel normal or restored. So when I look back, that’s the marker for me — around when those family responsibilities increased. Since then, for about fifteen months or so, it’s been there in the background pretty consistently.

I mean, I know I’ve been bothered by feeling flat, and there are moments where I get kind of discouraged about it, like, “Why can’t I just do the thing I normally do?” But if you mean am I walking around feeling actively sad or down all day, separate from the stuff I’ve lost interest in… I don’t know that I’ve noticed that clearly.\n\nIt feels more like an absence, if that makes sense. Like the volume is turned down. But I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m sad most of the time if that’s not exactly what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or low on energy lately, even when you’re not doing anything physically taxing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really tracked it as “energy” in a clear way. It’s more that I’ll look at something I normally would just do, even something not that demanding, and there’s this resistance to getting going. If I’m just sitting there not thinking about any of that, I don’t necessarily feel sad. I mean, I can get annoyed with myself when I don’t follow through on something the way I think I should, especially if it’s something simple or something I normally handle without making a big production out of it. But I don’t know that I’d call it feeling like I’m letting people down.\n\nLike, I’m not walking around thinking, “I’m a failure,” or assuming everyone’s disappointed in me. It’s more occasional self-criticism, I guess. I’ll have a thought like, “Come on, why is this taking you so long?” But then it sort of passes, and I can usually recognize that it’s just me being frustrated.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a big guilt thing or feeling worthless for no clear reason. If anything, it’s more mild frustration with myself than feeling like I’m not living up to some major standard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or that even small tasks take longer to complete because your thoughts or actions feel slowed down?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed that, no. Or at least not in a way where I’d say my movements or speech feel slowed down. I don’t think people have been telling me I’m talking slower, and I don’t feel like I’m physically dragging through the room or anything like that.\n\nSmall tasks can take longer sometimes, but I think that’s more because I hesitate getting started or I get stuck deciding how to approach them, not because my body or thoughts are moving in slow motion. Like once I’m actually doing the thing, I don’t usually feel slowed down. It’s more the lead-up that can stretch out.\n\nSo I’d say… no, I haven’t clearly noticed being slowed down in that specific sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately, like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major there. My appetite seems… mostly the same, at least as far as I can tell. I’m not skipping meals because food seems disgusting or anything like that, and I haven’t had that thing where my clothes are suddenly looser or I’m looking at the scale surprised.\n\nI mean, day to day it varies a little — some days I might eat lighter or get distracted and eat later than I meant to — but that feels pretty normal for me. I wouldn’t say I’m eating much less than usual, and I haven’t been trying to lose weight or noticing weight coming off. So, no, not really any clear appetite or weight change lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like photography walks or mentoring, don’t give you the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. It’s not that I suddenly hate those things or anything. It’s more like they’ve gone kind of flat on me.\n\nPhotography walks used to be one of those things that made me feel like myself. I’d notice light hitting a building a certain way, or I’d plan a route just because I knew there’d be interesting angles or people or shadows. Lately, even thinking about getting the camera ready feels heavy, like there’s this extra step between me and doing it. And if I do go, I’m kind of going through the motions. I’ll see something I know I would normally care about, and there’s just not much spark there.\n\nSame with mentoring the kids. I used to get a lot of satisfaction from that, like I was useful and present and connected. Now I still know it matters, intellectually, but I don’t feel pulled toward it the same way. I stop initiating. I don’t reach out as much. I’ll put it off and then feel weird about how little energy I have for something I genuinely value.\n\nEven smaller things, like keeping a clean calendar — and I know that sounds boring, but for me that’s always been part of feeling steady and competent — even that has started to feel like a chore instead of something that gives me a sense of order. So yes, the pleasure or satisfaction is dulled. Flat is probably the best word. Flat and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where your mood has felt persistently down or low — not just a passing feeling — and if so, how often has that happened over the last few weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to measure that, honestly. I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around every single day thinking, “I’m depressed” or “my mood is low,” in this very clear way. It’s more… some days feel dimmer than others, if that makes sense.\n\nOver the last few weeks, I’ve had stretches where I feel kind of heavy or discouraged for a good part of the day, but I have trouble separating whether that’s actually low mood or just being tired, or frustrated, or not feeling much from things. It’s not like a quick bad mood that passes in ten minutes, but I also don’t know that I’d call it constant.\n\nIf I had to guess, I’d say it’s happened on some days, maybe several, but I’m not confident enough to give you a clean number. I notice it more when I stop and think about how my day felt overall, rather than in the moment. So… yes, maybe there have been days like that, but I’m not sure how often exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re not good enough or that you’re falling short of who you feel like you should be, even when things aren’t going badly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. I mean, I can be self-critical, sure — especially if I forget something or don’t handle something as smoothly as I think I should. I’ve always had a bit of that “I should be more organized, I should be more on top of things” voice in my head.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling like I’m fundamentally not good enough, or like I’m failing as a person even when nothing is actually going wrong. That doesn’t really fit. If I feel bad about myself, it’s usually tied to something specific, like “I didn’t follow through on that,” not this broad sense that I’m worthless or falling short of who I’m supposed to be.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a major thing lately. At least not beyond normal frustration with myself here and there.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like photography walks or mentoring the kids, even when you do manage to do them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Even when I do manage to do them, it’s like the part of me that used to get something back from it just isn’t really coming online.\n\nLike with the photography walks, I can physically go out, I can bring the camera, I can notice things that I know are interesting — a reflection, a doorway, somebody standing in good light — but it doesn’t land the same way. It’s more like I’m observing myself doing the activity rather than actually enjoying it. And that’s strange, because that used to make me feel like myself.\n\nThe mentoring is similar. I still care about the kids, and I know it matters. It’s not that I think it’s pointless. But the satisfaction, the sense of connection or purpose I used to feel afterward, it’s muted. I’ll do it and then come home feeling more tired than fulfilled.\n\nAnd even something simple like keeping my calendar clean — which sounds small, but for me has always been a way of feeling steady and competent — that’s become heavy too. I’m not getting that little feeling of order or relief from it anymore.\n\nSo, yes. That’s really what made me come in. The things that usually anchor me or make me feel like myself started feeling flat and heavy, and then I noticed I was just not initiating them as much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you push yourself to do the things you used to enjoy, like photography walks or mentoring, they still feel meaningless or unfulfilling?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much it. I can still make myself do them sometimes, but they don’t give back what they used to.\n\nWith the photography walks, for example, I’ll go through the motions — take the camera, walk the same kinds of routes, notice little things I would normally want to capture — but it feels flat. Like I know, intellectually, “This is the kind of thing I enjoy,” but I’m not really feeling it. It’s almost heavy instead of restorative.\n\nAnd with mentoring the kids, I still know it matters, and I don’t want to sound like I don’t care, because I do. But the feeling afterward used to be, I don’t know, grounding? Like I was connected to something useful. Lately it’s more like I did the task and then I’m just drained.\n\nEven keeping my calendar clean — which is a small thing, but it usually makes me feel like myself, like I have some order — has started to feel like another weight. That’s actually what made me come in: the things that normally anchor me started feeling flat and heavy, and then I noticed I wasn’t even starting them as much.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this sense of things feeling flat and heavy, and not getting much pleasure from the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I wanted to admit at first. I’d say about a year and three months now, give or take.\n\nIt wasn’t like I woke up one day and everything felt flat. It kind of crept in after my family member’s health and behavior started changing, and I ended up taking on more of the coordination around that — appointments, calls, keeping track of things, trying to make sure nothing fell through the cracks. At first I thought, “Okay, I’m just tired, this is a stressful period,” and I kept telling myself it would pass once things settled.\n\nBut it didn’t really settle, and after a while I noticed that the heaviness wasn’t just about the coordination itself. It was bleeding into the things that usually helped me feel normal or restored. So when I look back, that’s the marker for me — around when those family responsibilities increased. Since then, for about fifteen months or so, it’s been there in the background pretty consistently.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… not like this, no. I’ve had stretches where I was tired or stressed and didn’t feel as interested in things, but this feels different in the way it’s sort of spread across everything that usually makes me feel like me.

What made me come in is that the normal anchors — my photography walks, mentoring the kids, even something as basic as keeping my calendar clean — they all started feeling flat and heavy. And it wasn’t just that I was busy and couldn’t get to them. I actually stopped initiating them as much. I’d think about going out with the camera and then just… not. Or I’d have mentoring on the schedule and I’d do it, but I wouldn’t get that sense afterward that I used to get.

So I guess I’ve had low patches before, but this particular kind of flatness, where the things that normally pull me back into myself don’t really work and I’m not even reaching for them the same way — that’s what feels newer, and that’s what worried me enough to come in.
